function getParameterByName(name, url) {
		if(url == null){
		  url = window.location.href;
		}
	    name = name.replace(/[\[\]]/g, '\\$&');
	    var regex = new RegExp('[?&]' + name + '(=([^&#]*)|&|#|$)'),
		results = regex.exec(url);
	    if (!results) return null;
	    if (!results[2]) return '';
	    return decodeURIComponent(results[2].replace(/\+/g, ' '));
	}

    ;(function(win, doc, style, timeout) {
    var STYLE_ID = 'at-body-style';
    function getParent() {
    return doc.getElementsByTagName('head')[0];
    }
    function addStyle(parent, id, def) {
        if (!parent) {
        return;
        }
        var style = doc.createElement('style');
        style.id = id;
        style.innerHTML = def;
        parent.appendChild(style);
    }
    function removeStyle(parent, id) {
        if (!parent) {
            return;
        }
        var style = doc.getElementById(id);
        if (!style) {
            return;
        }
        parent.removeChild(style);
    }
    addStyle(getParent(), STYLE_ID, style);
    setTimeout(function() {
    removeStyle(getParent(), STYLE_ID);
    }, timeout);

 * String.prototype.replaceAll() polyfill
 * https://gomakethings.com/how-to-replace-a-section-of-a-string-with-another-one-with-vanilla-js/

	String.prototype.replaceAll = function(str, newStr){
		// If a regex pattern
		if (Object.prototype.toString.call(str).toLowerCase() === '[object regexp]') {
			return this.replace(str, newStr);
		}
		// If a string
		return this.replace(new RegExp(str, 'g'), newStr);
	};

	function getCookie(cname) {
	 	var name = cname + "=";
  		var decodedCookie = decodeURIComponent(document.cookie);
		var ca = decodedCookie.split(';');
  		for(var i = 0; i <ca.length; i++) {
    			var c = ca[i];
    			while (c.charAt(0) == ' ') {
     				 c = c.substring(1);
    			}
    			if (c.indexOf(name) == 0) {
    				 return c.substring(name.length, c.length);
    			}
  		}
  		return "";
	}

function addStyle(styles) {            
    /* Create style document */
    var css = document.createElement('style');
    css.type = 'text/css';

    if (css.styleSheet) 
        css.styleSheet.cssText = styles;
    else 
        css.appendChild(document.createTextNode(styles));

    /* Append style to the tag name */
    document.getElementsByTagName("head")[0].appendChild(css);
}
function httpGet(theUrl,req_type) {
    if (window.XMLHttpRequest) { // code for IE7+, Firefox, Chrome, Opera, Safari
        xmlhttp=new XMLHttpRequest();
    } else {// code for IE6, IE5
        xmlhttp=new ActiveXObject("Microsoft.XMLHTTP");
    }
    xmlhttp.onreadystatechange=function()
    {
        if (xmlhttp.readyState==4 && xmlhttp.status==200)
        {
            if(req_type == 'menu_html'){
            var nav_text = xmlhttp.responseText.replaceAll('https://static.cloud.coveo.com/searchui/v2.9159/js/CoveoJsSearch.Lazy.min.js', '');
            nav_text = nav_text.replaceAll('src="/', 'src="'+maindomain_lang+'/');
            nav_text = nav_text.replaceAll("src='/", "src='"+maindomain_lang+"/");
            nav_text = nav_text.replaceAll("'/content", "'"+maindomain_lang+"/content");
                        
            document.getElementById("PAN_2021_NAV_ASYNC").innerHTML = nav_text.replaceAll('href="/', 'href="'+maindomain_lang+'/');

            var lozad_back = document.getElementsByClassName('lozad-background');
            Array.prototype.forEach.call(lozad_back, function(el) {
            // Do stuff here
            var el_back_img_path = el.getAttribute('data-background-image');
            var first_pos = el_back_img_path.indexOf("'");
            var last_pos = el_back_img_path.indexOf("'",first_pos+1);
            el_back_img_path = el_back_img_path.substring(first_pos+1,last_pos);
            let result = el_back_img_path.includes(main_site_url);
            if(result == false){
                el.setAttribute("data-background-image",main_site_url+el_back_img_path);
            }
            else{
                el.setAttribute("data-background-image",el_back_img_path);
            }
            });
            const observer_lozad = lozad('.lozad, .lozad-background'); // lazy loads elements with default selector as '.lozad'
            observer_lozad.observe();
            }
            if(req_type == 'head_inline_css'){
            addStyle(xmlhttp.responseText);
            }
        }
    }
    xmlhttp.open("GET", theUrl, true );
    xmlhttp.send();    
}

    <section class="section blog-contents"> 
      <div class="pa blog-editor">
        <div class="l-container">
          <div class="be__wrapper">
            <div class="be__contents">
              <div class="be__contents-wrapper">
                <h2><a id="post-186732-_4lt92rr5muov"></a>Executive Summary</h2>
<p>This research demonstrates post-exploitation techniques that could allow an attacker with root access on a compromised Kubernetes node to misuse an open standard and reference implementation for machine identity known as SPIFFE/SPIRE to impersonate co-located workloads and harvest SPIFFE Verifiable Identity Documents (SVIDs). We show how the trust assumption at the core of every machine-identity system — that the node is trusted — collapses once an attacker obtains root on that node. Unit 42 has not observed this technique exploited in the wild.</p>
<p>The Secure Production Identity Framework for Everyone (SPIFFE)/the SPIFFE Runtime Environment (SPIRE) is widely deployed in Kubernetes and cloud-native environments to replace long-lived secrets with short-lived, cryptographically verifiable workload identities.</p>
<p>Our research shows how an attacker with root can spoof the Linux control group (cgroup) information the SPIRE agent uses during workload attestation. This tricks the agent into issuing a co-located workload's SVID to an attacker-controlled process.</p>
<p>As part of this research, we developed <a href="https://github.com/PaloAltoNetworks/spooffe" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Spooffe</a>, an open-source tool that defenders can use to test whether an attacker with administrative access could manipulate cgroup metadata to retrieve co-located workload identities and assess the resulting identity area of impact.</p>
<p>When designing threat models for SPIFFE/SPIRE, organizations should assume that root-level access to a node grants access to all cryptographic identities scoped to it. We recommend performing the following activities to reduce exposure:</p>
<ul>
<li>Harden nodes</li>
<li>Restrict root access</li>
<li>Prohibit privileged containers, host access</li>
<li>Minimize reliance on weak selectors</li>
</ul>
<p>Palo Alto Networks customers are better protected from the threats described here through the following products and services:</p>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/cortex/cortex-xdr?_gl=1*13pmp8e*_ga*NzQyNjM2NzkuMTY2NjY3OTczNw..*_ga_KS2MELEEFC*MTY2OTczNjA2MS4zMS4wLjE2Njk3MzYwNjEuNjAuMC4w" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Cortex XDR</a> and <a href="https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/resources/datasheets/cortex-xsiam-aag" target="_blank" rel="noopener">XSIAM</a></li>
<li><a href="https://cortex-docs.paloaltonetworks.com/cortex-cloud-runtime-security/cortex-cloud-identity-security/what-is-cortex-cloud-identity-security" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Cortex Cloud Identity Threat Detection</a></li>
</ul>
<p>If you think you might have been compromised or have an urgent matter, contact the<a href="https://start.paloaltonetworks.com/contact-unit42.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"> Unit 42 Incident Response team</a>.</p>

<h2><a id="post-186732-_wven14kmgum2"></a>Introduction</h2>
<p><a href="https://spiffe.io/docs/latest/spiffe-about/overview/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">SPIFFE</a> is an open standard for machine identity designed to solve the “Secret Zero” problem — the challenge of securely introducing the initial secret required to bootstrap trust — by replacing long-lived secrets with short-lived workload identities. When deployed correctly, SPIFFE enforces strong identity boundaries between workloads.</p>
<p>However, these guarantees rely on a core assumption shared by all identity systems that the underlying node is trusted. If an attacker gains root access to a node, they can interact with identity mechanisms to retrieve all identities authorized to that compromised node.</p>
<p>Our research explores how attackers can exploit root access to harvest workload identities from a compromised node. In this post, we lay the groundwork by explaining machine identity and how SPIFFE establishes and verifies trust in cloud-native environments. We then demonstrate workload impersonation through selector spoofing. Finally, we introduce <a href="https://github.com/PaloAltoNetworks/spooffe" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Spooffe</a>, a tool we built to automate the extraction of these workload identities (SVIDs).</p>
<p>Note to readers: If you’re already familiar with SPIFFE/SPIRE concepts and architecture, you can jump directly to <a href="#workload-attestation">Workload Attestation</a> and <a href="#attests-workload">How the Agent Attests the Workload</a> sections.</p>
<h2><a id="post-186732-_c2iylqdxcoj7"></a>SPIFFE Overview</h2>
<p>Consider a scenario where two applications, a frontend and a backend, must communicate securely.</p>
<p>We could generate key pairs and exchange public keys to communicate through Mutual Transport Layer Security (mTLS<em>)</em>, but this option raises a few key questions:</p>
<ul>
<li>Who rotates those keys?</li>
<li>Who revokes them if the app is compromised?</li>
<li>Can we verify who/what is presenting the keys?</li>
</ul>
<p>SPIFFE addresses these issues by standardizing how machines are named and how short-lived credentials are issued.</p>
<p>The term machines refers to two broad categories:</p>
<ul>
<li>Workloads: Containers, processes and services running application logic</li>
<li>Devices: Endpoints such as desktops, mobile devices and internet of things (IoT) or operational technology (OT) systems
<ul>
<li>(Note: Device identity is not part of the core SPIFFE specification)</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
<h2><a id="post-186732-_suudn5b5j2nx"></a>SPIFFE Identity Components</h2>
<p>Each workload is assigned three identity components:</p>
<ol>
<li>SPIFFE ID: Who you are (your name)</li>
<li>SPIFFE Verifiable Identity (SVID)<strong>: </strong>Proof that you are who you are claiming to be (your credentials)</li>
<li>Trust Bundle: How others verify that a trusted authority issued your credential</li>
</ol>
<p>Let’s go into a little more detail about each of these.</p>
<p>SPIFFE ID is a canonical name for a workload identity that follows a URI-style format like <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">spiffe://&lt;trust-domain&gt;/&lt;path&gt;</span> (Figure 1).</p>
<figure id="attachment_186744" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-186744" style="width: 900px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-186744 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-754351-186732-2.png" alt="A diagram illustrating a SPIFFE URI. The structure includes three parts: &quot;URI scheme,&quot; &quot;trust domain name,&quot; and &quot;name or identity of the specific workload&quot;. Arrows point from each label to the corresponding parts of the URI." width="900" height="612" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-754351-186732-2.png 1280w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-754351-186732-2-647x440.png 647w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-754351-186732-2-1029x700.png 1029w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-754351-186732-2-768x523.png 768w" sizes="(max-width: 900px) 100vw, 900px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-186744" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 1. SPIFFE ID.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The middle part (<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">example[.]com</span>) in Figure 1 is the trust domain, the issuer of identity that acts as a security boundary.</p>
<p>In this way, workloads can have an identity and know who to communicate with.</p>
<p>However, identity alone does not guarantee trust or security, so we add the SPIFFE Verifiable Identity Document (SVID). This is a short-lived credential that a workload presents to prove its identity. It is cryptographically signed by the certificate authority (CA) server and always includes the workload's SPIFFE ID.</p>
<p>SVIDs support two primary formats:</p>
<ul>
<li>X.509 SVID: A certificate with an embedded public key, typically used for mTLS</li>
<li>JSON Web Token (JWT) SVID<strong>:</strong> A signed JWT token used as a bearer token for application-level authorization</li>
</ul>
<p>Finally, we have the trust bundle, which is a set of trust anchors — root CA certificates or JSON Web Key Sets (JWKS). These are used to verify that a trusted authority issued an SVID within a trust domain.</p>
<p>To understand how these identity components are issued and verified in practice, we first need to look at the SPIRE architecture and its core runtime components.</p>
<h2><a id="post-186732-_a3diahkwv4un"></a>SPIRE Architecture</h2>
<p><a href="https://github.com/spiffe/spire" target="_blank" rel="noopener">SPIRE</a> is a production-ready implementation of the SPIFFE specification. While <a href="https://spiffe.io/docs/latest/spiffe-about/overview/#open-source-software-that-implements-spiffe" target="_blank" rel="noopener">several</a> implementations exist, we chose SPIRE for this research because it is widely deployed in Kubernetes environments and fully implements the SPIFFE standard. Furthermore, because SPIRE is open source, we can inspect its internals to understand how the specification works in practice.</p>
<p>SPIRE is composed of a few simple pieces (as shown in Figure 2 below):</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<figure id="attachment_186755" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-186755" style="width: 900px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-186755 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-756774-186732-3.png" alt="A diagram illustrating SPIFFE/SPIRE architecture. At the top, &quot;CLI Tool&quot; and &quot;API Calls&quot; lead to a &quot;registration API&quot; and &quot;Trust bundle&quot; within a server. Below the server, &quot;Node API&quot; connects to two agents, each with a &quot;WL API.&quot; A highlighted entry shows components and related terms." width="900" height="920" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-756774-186732-3.png 1332w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-756774-186732-3-430x440.png 430w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-756774-186732-3-685x700.png 685w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-756774-186732-3-768x785.png 768w" sizes="(max-width: 900px) 100vw, 900px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-186755" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 2. SPIFFE/SPIRE architecture.</figcaption></figure>
<ul>
<li>Workload: A single piece of software deployed to do a specific job (e.g., a process, container, pod)</li>
<li>SPIRE Server (control plane): This is the CA that stores registration entries. It also issues and cryptographically signs SVIDs. Additionally, it publishes the trust bundle for the trust domain.</li>
<li>SPIRE Agent: This runs on every compute node (e.g., a Kubernetes node, VM or bare-metal host). It performs the following activities:
<ul>
<li>Accepts requests from workloads locally over the Workload API (UNIX socket)</li>
<li>Communicates with the server via the Node API</li>
<li>Performs attestation</li>
<li>Caches SVIDs</li>
<li>Handles rotation</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>Registration entries and selectors: These are server-side policy objects that define which workloads are allowed to receive which SPIFFE IDs. When a workload requests an identity, the SPIRE agent collects runtime attributes (selectors) about the workload and the server compares them against registration entries to determine which identity, if any, should be issued.</li>
</ul>
<p>With the SPIRE architecture in mind, we can now walk through how workload-to-workload identity verification works end to end.</p>
<h2><a id="post-186732-_yr0oeay2cjc4"></a>Workload-to-Workload Identity Verification Flow</h2>
<p>When workload A needs to communicate with workload B over mTLS, it requests a short-lived credential from the local SPIRE agent. The agent attests the workload and forwards the attestation data to the SPIFFE server. Based on predefined registration policies, the server selects the appropriate SPIFFE ID for the workload and issues a short-lived X.509 SVID.</p>
<p>The server also publishes the corresponding public keys as part of the trust bundle.</p>
<p>When workload A initiates a connection, it presents its SVID during the mTLS handshake. Workload B verifies the SVID by validating the signature against the trust bundle, checking the certificate’s expiration, and confirming that the SPIFFE ID matches an expected identity.</p>
<p>If these checks succeed, workload B can cryptographically authenticate workload A and establish a secure connection. This enables workload-to-workload communication based on identity rather than long-lived secrets (Figure 3).</p>
<figure id="attachment_186766" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-186766" style="width: 900px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-186766 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-759438-186732-4.png" alt="A diagram depicting a process flow between two workloads, A and B, using secure communication. Workload A signs data with a server's private key, creating an SVID (X.509) that includes a public key. This information is verified by Workload B using a trust bundle. The process includes verification and the exchange of digital certificates via an endpoint. The visual features robots and padlock icons to represent processes and security." width="900" height="412" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-759438-186732-4.png 1427w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-759438-186732-4-786x360.png 786w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-759438-186732-4-768x352.png 768w" sizes="(max-width: 900px) 100vw, 900px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-186766" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 3. Workload communication.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The identity flow described above depends on attestation, the process by which SPIRE determines whether a workload is allowed to receive an identity. SPIRE performs attestation at two levels:</p>
<ol>
<li>Node attestation establishes trust in the agent running on a node</li>
<li>Workload attestation determines the identity of individual workloads</li>
</ol>
<p>In this post, we focus on workload attestation, as it is the mechanism directly involved in selector evaluation and the attacks discussed later.</p>
<h2><a id="workload-attestation"></a>Workload Attestation</h2>
<p>Before the agent attests the workloads, the SPIRE server’s administrator must register the workload selectors in the SPIRE server so it can later compare them to the selectors in the agent.</p><!-- Crayon Syntax Highlighter v_2.7.2_beta -->

</span><span class="crayon-sy">\</span></div><div class="crayon-line" id="crayon-6aa61383ae3f0706496254-3"><span class="crayon-h">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</span>-<span class="crayon-e">spiffeID <!-- [Format Time: 0.0007 seconds] -->
<p>In this Kubernetes example, the registration entry authorizes any pod running in the <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">default</span> namespace and using the <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">default</span> service account to receive the specified SPIFFE identity (spiffeID).</p>
<p>The resulting record is stored on the SPIRE server as follows:</p><!-- Crayon Syntax Highlighter v_2.7.2_beta -->

<!-- [Format Time: 0.0003 seconds] -->
<p>The SPIRE agent periodically synchronizes and caches these registration entries from the server, using them locally during workload attestation to determine which identity applies. The agent generates key pairs for each registration entry, sends certificate signing requests (CSRs) to the server and caches the resulting SVIDs.</p>
<p>When a workload wants to authenticate, it requests an identity from the agent over the Workload API (Figure 4, step 1). The agent performs workload attestation (Figure 4, step 2) by gathering selectors from the workload process and matching them against cached registration entries.</p>
<p>Upon a successful match (Figure 4, step 3), the agent returns:</p>
<ul>
<li>X.509 SVID</li>
<li>Private key</li>
<li>Trust bundle</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> This example is based on an X.509 SVID request. For JWT SVID requests, the agent returns only a signed JWT token.</p>
<figure id="attachment_186777" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-186777" style="width: 1000px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-186777 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-761797-186732-5.png" alt="A diagram titled &quot;Workload Attestation&quot; illustrating the process flow. &quot;Workload A&quot; connects to &quot;kubelet&quot; and &quot;Linux&quot; components, then to &quot;k8s&quot; and &quot;UNIX,&quot; leading to the &quot;Agent.&quot; The &quot;Agent&quot; includes &quot;Workload Attestor&quot; and connects to the &quot;Workload API.&quot; Cached entries and SVIDs are shown. The process is numbered 1 to 3, showing the flow of requests and responses." width="1000" height="482" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-761797-186732-5.png 1427w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-761797-186732-5-786x379.png 786w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-761797-186732-5-768x370.png 768w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-186777" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 4. Workload attestation.</figcaption></figure>
<p>With the high-level flow in mind, we can now examine how attestation works in practice.</p>
<h2><a id="attests-workload"></a>How the Agent Attests the Workload</h2>
<p>When a workload requests an SVID (via <a href="https://github.com/spiffe/spire/blob/e904ad991fe671c5a372251aa89a886d8474e876/pkg/agent/endpoints/workload/handler.go#L65" target="_blank" rel="noopener">FetchJWTSVID</a> or <a href="https://github.com/spiffe/spire/blob/e904ad991fe671c5a372251aa89a886d8474e876/pkg/agent/endpoints/workload/handler.go#L214" target="_blank" rel="noopener">FetchX509SVID</a>), it connects to the agent Workload API, typically via a Unix domain socket (such as <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/run/spire/sockets/agent.sock</span>). The agent then <a href="https://github.com/spiffe/spire/blob/e904ad991fe671c5a372251aa89a886d8474e876/pkg/common/peertracker/uds_linux.go#L9-L22" target="_blank" rel="noopener">extracts</a> the PID for the calling process.</p>
<p>Once the agent receives the PID, it passes it to the configured workload attestor plugins, which collect selectors based on process and container metadata. SPIRE agents support several workload-attestor plugins. Common plugins include docker, k8s, systemd, Unix and Windows. In our cluster, the agent uses the <a href="https://github.com/spiffe/spire/blob/e904ad991fe671c5a372251aa89a886d8474e876/pkg/agent/plugin/workloadattestor/k8s/k8s.go" target="_blank" rel="noopener">k8s</a> and <a href="https://github.com/spiffe/spire/blob/e904ad991fe671c5a372251aa89a886d8474e876/pkg/agent/plugin/workloadattestor/unix/unix_posix.go" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Unix</a> plugins.</p>
<h3><a id="post-186732-_dgvlz7padja0"></a>Kubernetes Plugin (k8s)</h3>
<p>The k8s plugin uses the workload PID to access <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/proc/&lt;pid&gt;/mountinfo <span style="font-family: georgia, palatino, serif;">or</span> /proc/&lt;pid&gt;/cgroups</span>. It calls <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;"><a href="https://github.com/spiffe/spire/blob/e904ad991fe671c5a372251aa89a886d8474e876/pkg/agent/plugin/workloadattestor/k8s/k8s_posix.go#L58C27-L58C50" target="_blank" rel="noopener">GetPodUIDAndContainerID</a></span> to extract the pod UID and the container ID. In our environment, this process looks like the following:</p><!-- Crayon Syntax Highlighter v_2.7.2_beta -->

		<div id="crayon-6aa61383ae3fd596549663" class="crayon-syntax crayon-theme-classic crayon-font-monaco crayon-os-pc print-yes notranslate" data-settings=" minimize scroll-mouseover wrap" style=" margin-top: 12px; margin-bottom: 12px; font-size: 12px !important; line-height: 15px !important;">

class="crayon-e">.slice</span>/<span class="crayon-i">cri</span>-<span class="crayon-i">containerd</span>-<span class="crayon-h">&lt;</span><span class="crayon-i">container_id</span><span 				</table>
			</div>
		</div>
<!-- [Format Time: 0.0003 seconds] -->
<p>After extracting the container ID and pod UID, the k8s plugin queries the kubelet to retrieve pod metadata. To do this, it uses the SPIRE agent’s service account token, stored at <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token</span>.</p>
<p>The agent's service account has the following permissions, which allow it to list pods and access node information:</p><!-- Crayon Syntax Highlighter v_2.7.2_beta -->

		<div id="crayon-6aa61383ae3ff699405270" class="crayon-syntax crayon-theme-classic crayon-font-monaco crayon-os-pc print-yes notranslate" data-settings=" minimize scroll-mouseover wrap" style=" margin-top: 12px; margin-bottom: 12px; font-size: 12px !important; line-height: 15px !important;">

  name: spire-agent-cluster-role

<!-- [Format Time: 0.0001 seconds] -->
<p>Using this token, the plugin calls <a href="https://github.com/spiffe/spire/blob/e904ad991fe671c5a372251aa89a886d8474e876/pkg/agent/plugin/workloadattestor/k8s/k8s.go#L321" target="_blank" rel="noopener">getPodList</a> to retrieve all pods on the node. It then identifies the pod whose UID and container ID match the values extracted from the /proc directory. Once matched, it <a href="https://github.com/spiffe/spire/blob/e904ad991fe671c5a372251aa89a886d8474e876/pkg/agent/plugin/workloadattestor/k8s/k8s.go#L799" target="_blank" rel="noopener">collects selectors</a> from the pod's metadata:</p><!-- Crayon Syntax Highlighter v_2.7.2_beta -->

<p></p>
<h3><a id="post-186732-_ox7e562wzoec"></a>Unix Plugin</h3>
<p>The Unix plugin gathers information from <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/proc</span> to produce selectors such as user identifier (UID) and group identifier (GID). These selectors are used during workload attestation to bind a workload’s identity to operating system level properties of the calling process, helping SPIRE distinguish between different workloads running on the same node.</p>
<p>Here is an example of the selectors it <a href="https://github.com/spiffe/spire/blob/e904ad991fe671c5a372251aa89a886d8474e876/pkg/agent/plugin/workloadattestor/unix/unix_posix.go#L146-L201" target="_blank" rel="noopener">collects</a>:</p><!-- Crayon Syntax Highlighter v_2.7.2_beta -->

		<div id="crayon-6aa61383ae402163100124" class="crayon-syntax crayon-theme-classic crayon-font-monaco crayon-os-pc print-yes notranslate" data-settings=" minimize scroll-mouseover wrap" style=" margin-top: 12px; margin-bottom: 12px; font-size: 12px !important; line-height: 15px !important;">

<!-- [Format Time: 0.0002 seconds] -->
<p>The agent combines these selectors from both the k8s and Unix plugins. It then matches this selector set against the locally cached registration entries. If an entry's selectors are a subset of the workload's selectors, the agent returns the corresponding cached SVID to the workload.</p>
<h2><a id="post-186732-_gzoj6nuo5s55"></a>Workload Impersonation: Selector Spoofing via Cgroup</h2>
<p>This attack requires root-level access to the node. Our analysis revealed that workload attestation relies heavily on the workload’s cgroup path. An attacker with root access on the node can manipulate this cgroup path, potentially tricking the agent into believing the attestation claims belong to a different workload and obtain that workload’s identity. This could allow the attacker to impersonate the victim workload and access any services or resources trusted under that identity.</p>
<p>As a first step, we created a registration entry for a pod named <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">workload-a</span> in the server and we verified that we could fetch its identity from the pod:</p><!-- Crayon Syntax Highlighter v_2.7.2_beta -->

		<div id="crayon-6aa61383ae404912466169" class="crayon-syntax crayon-theme-classic crayon-font-monaco crayon-os-pc print-yes notranslate" data-settings=" minimize scroll-mouseover wrap" style=" margin-top: 12px; margin-bottom: 12px; font-size: 12px !important; line-height: 15px !important;">

<p>We can also see the <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">workload-a</span> SPIFFE ID inside the JWT token:</p><!-- Crayon Syntax Highlighter v_2.7.2_beta -->

		<div id="crayon-6aa61383ae40d497415576" class="crayon-syntax crayon-theme-classic crayon-font-monaco crayon-os-pc print-yes notranslate" data-settings=" minimize scroll-mouseover wrap" style=" margin-top: 12px; margin-bottom: 12px; font-size: 12px !important; line-height: 15px !important;">

<!-- [Format Time: 0.0000 seconds] -->
<p>The manual spoofing demonstration highlights that the security model relies entirely on a single, vulnerable assumption of node integrity. When we have root access, we can get the identity of any workload on the node.</p>
<p>This manual spoofing demonstration highlights a critical trust assumption in workload attestation. When an attacker has root-level access to a node, they can manipulate cgroup information to cause the SPIRE agent to misattribute workload identities. Under these conditions, the attacker can impersonate other workloads running on the same node and obtain their identities.</p>
<h2><a id="post-186732-_irry2x2qv2jh"></a>Spooffe</h2>
<p>These findings motivated us to develop <a href="https://github.com/PaloAltoNetworks/spooffe" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Spooffe</a>, a tool that allows defenders to automate selector spoofing to retrieve all the workload identities from the node.</p>
<p>Spooffe scans the node for running workloads, discovers their cgroup paths, and replicates them as a mock cgroup for its own process. It then queries the local SPIRE agent for the resulting identities (SVIDs), allowing us to collect all workload identities present on the host (Figure 5).</p>
<figure id="attachment_186788" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-186788" style="width: 900px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-186788 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-764051-186732-6.png" alt="A screenshot of a terminal displaying a command line output related to Kubernetes. The text includes creation and verification of a fake group, fetched JWTs, and viewing various certificates. It features commands like &#96;spoofre&#96; and directories related to Kubernetes pods." width="900" height="585" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-764051-186732-6.png 950w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-764051-186732-6-676x440.png 676w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/word-image-764051-186732-6-768x500.png 768w" sizes="(max-width: 900px) 100vw, 900px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-186788" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 5: Dumping all workloads' identities (SVIDs).</figcaption></figure>
<p>Besides selector spoofing, Spooffe includes additional capabilities. However, in this post we focus only on the features relevant to this research.</p>
<p>One such capability is agent impersonation, which examines whether an attacker can impersonate the SPIRE agent and communicate directly with the server to extract workload identities.</p>
<h2><a id="post-186732-_2an8ryq91inv"></a>Conclusion</h2>
<p>In this article, we demonstrated how an attacker with root access on a compromised node can misuse SPIFFE/SPIRE workload attestation to impersonate co-located workloads and retrieve their identities. By manipulating cgroup metadata, we showed how attackers could mislead the SPIRE agent into issuing valid SVIDs to an attacker-controlled process. We also introduced <a href="https://github.com/PaloAltoNetworks/spooffe" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Spooffe</a>, a tool that automates this technique to enumerate and extract workload identities from a node.</p>
<p>These findings highlight a fundamental assumption in workload identity systems: trust in the underlying node. While SPIFFE/SPIRE enforces strong cryptographic identity guarantees between workloads, those guarantees rely on the integrity of the environment where attestation occurs. Once that trust boundary is broken, identity isolation between workloads collapses, allowing attackers to move laterally using legitimate credentials rather than stolen secrets.</p>
<p>Organizations adopting workload identity should treat node-level compromise as equivalent to compromise of all identities scoped to that node. To reduce risk, restrict privileged containers, limit direct host access and minimize reliance on weak or easily spoofable selectors.</p>
<p>Palo Alto Networks has shared our findings with our fellow Cyber Threat Alliance (CTA) members. CTA members use this intelligence to rapidly deploy protections to their customers and to systematically disrupt malicious cyber actors. Learn more about the <a href="https://www.cyberthreatalliance.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Cyber Threat Alliance</a>.</p>
<h2><a id="post-186732-_lqzcx8cug942"></a>Palo Alto Networks Product Protections</h2>
<p>Palo Alto Networks customers are better protected from the threats discussed above through the following products:</p>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://docs-cortex.paloaltonetworks.com/p/XDR" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Cortex XDR</a> and <a href="https://docs-cortex.paloaltonetworks.com/p/XSIAM" target="_blank" rel="noopener">XSIAM</a> can help protect against post-exploitation activities using the multi-layer protection approach. This approach combines several layers of protection, including <a href="https://docs.paloaltonetworks.com/wildfire" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Advanced WildFire</a>, Behavioral Threat Protection, and <a href="https://docs-cortex.paloaltonetworks.com/r/Cortex-XDR/Cortex-XDR-3.x-Documentation/Malware-protection" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Endpoint Protection Modules</a> (EPM).</li>
<li><a href="https://cortex-docs.paloaltonetworks.com/cortex-cloud-runtime-security/cortex-cloud-identity-security/what-is-cortex-cloud-identity-security" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Cortex Cloud Identity Threat Detection </a>can help deliver end-to-end visibility across cloud providers and IdPs by baselining real-time access patterns. By continuously monitoring these behaviors, ITDR detects identity abuse and compromised credentials targeting critical cloud resources and Kubernetes workloads, automatically triggering containment actions to keep attackers out.</li>
</ul>

<script>
 var PAN_LOAD_RECAPTCHA_LANGUAGE = 'en_US';

(function($) {
	// Migrated from the unit42-v5 + Modifications
	var subscribeSuccess = false;
	var email = document.getElementById('newsletter-email');
	var subscription_form = document.getElementById('unit42footerSubscription_form');
	var subscription_form_button = document.getElementById('unit42footerSubscription_form_button');
        var isCaptchaComplete02  = false;	
        window.captchaInlineFormOnload = function(token){
            isCaptchaComplete02 = true;
            $("form[name='Unit42_Subscribe'] button[id='unit42footerSubscription_form_button']").prop('disabled', false);
        }
        var PAN_INLINE_FORM_RECAPTCHA_LOADED = false;
        const SITE_KEY = '6LdDKhIsAAAAABdoYV3PR5VJ2Gz23_dLSvq2uvBQ'; 
                if(!PAN_INLINE_FORM_RECAPTCHA_LOADED){
               PAN_INLINE_FORM_RECAPTCHA_LOADED = true;
               // trigger loading api.js (recaptcha.js) script
               var recapthaScript = $("script[src='https://www.google.com/recaptcha/enterprise.js?render="+SITE_KEY+"&hl="+PAN_LOAD_RECAPTCHA_LANGUAGE+"&onload=captchaInlineFormOnload']").length
               // console.log("recapthaScript",recapthaScript);
               if (!recapthaScript) {
                   var head = document.getElementsByTagName('head')[0];
                   var script = document.createElement('script');
                   script.typerecapthaScript = 'text/javascript';
                   script.src = "https://www.google.com/recaptcha/enterprise.js?render="+SITE_KEY+"&hl="+PAN_LOAD_RECAPTCHA_LANGUAGE+"&onload=captchaInlineFormOnload";
                   head.appendChild(script);
               }
               else{
                   captchaInlineFormOnload();
                }
           }
            	
	
	$(subscription_form).submit(function(e) {
		e.preventDefault();
		e.stopImmediatePropagation();
		updateEmailMask();
		var success = true;
		var form = $(this);
		var mail = form.find('input[name="Email"]');
		
		if (mail.val() === '') {
			mail.addClass('has-error');
			showError(1);
			success = false;	
		}  else if (!isEmail(mail.val())){
			showError(2);
			success = false;
		}
		else {
			mail.removeClass('has-error');
			$('.error-mail').addClass('d-none');
		}

		

		if (success) {
                                                if(grecaptcha){
                        grecaptcha.enterprise.ready(function() {
                            grecaptcha.enterprise.execute(SITE_KEY, {action: 'submit'}).then(function(token) {
                                submitSubscribeForm({token :token, action : 'submit'},form);
                            });
                        });
                    }
                    			
		}
		return false;
	});
        function submitSubscribeForm(tokenData, form){
            var serialized_data = form.serialize();
            let captchaParams = `&g-recaptcha-response=${tokenData.token}&action=${tokenData.action}`;
            $.ajax({
				type: 'POST',
				url: form.attr('action'),
				data: serialized_data+captchaParams,
				beforeSend: function() {
                 form.find('button').addClass('is-loading');
    			},
				success: function(msg) {
					form.find('.form-success-message').html('<p class="success-message">You have been successfully subscribed</p>');
					form.find('button').removeClass('is-loading');
					$(email).val('');
					clearError();
				},
				error: function(jqXHR, textStatus, errorThrown) {
					$(subscription_form_button).addClass('is-disabled', true);
					form.find('button').removeClass('is-loading');
					
				}
			});
            
        }
	function showError(error_type){
		if(error_type == 1) {
			$('.error-mail').text("Please enter the email address.").addClass('error-show');
			$(subscription_form_button).addClass('is-disabled');
		} else if(error_type == 2){
			$('.error-mail').text("Please provide a valid e-mail address.").addClass('error-show');
			$(subscription_form_button).addClass('is-disabled');
		}	
		$(subscription_form_button).removeClass('is-loading');
	}
	function clearError(){
		$('.error-mail').text("").removeClass('error-show');;
		$(subscription_form_button).removeClass('is-loading');
		$(subscription_form_button).removeClass('is-disabled');
	}
	
	$(email).on('input', function (event) { 
		var email = $(this).val();
		if (isEmail(email) ) {			
			clearError();
		} else if(email == ""){
			clearError();
		} else{
			showError(2);			  
		}
    });

	function isEmail(email) {
		var re = /^(([^<>()\[\]\\.,;:\s@"]+(\.[^<>()\[\]\\.,;:\s@"]+)*)|(".+"))@((\[[0-9]{1,3}\.[0-9]{1,3}\.[0-9]{1,3}\.[0-9]{1,3}\])|(([a-zA-Z\-0-9]+\.)+[a-zA-Z]{2,}))$/;
		return re.test(String(email).toLowerCase());
	}
	
	
	
	function updateEmailMask() {
		var email = $("#unit42footerSubscription_form input[name='Email']").val();
		if (email && email.trim() != '') {
			var maskedEmail = maskEmailAddress(email);
			$("#unit42footerSubscription_form input[name='emailFormMask']").val(maskedEmail);
		}
	}

	function maskEmailAddress (emailAddress) {
		function mask(str) {
			var strLen = str.length;
			if (strLen > 4) {
				return str.substr(0, 1) + str.substr(1, strLen - 1).replace(/\w/g, '*') + str.substr(-1,1);
			}
			return str.replace(/\w/g, '*');
		}
		return emailAddress.replace(/([\w.]+)@([\w.]+)(\.[\w.]+)/g, function (m, p1, p2, p3) {
			return mask(p1) + '@' + mask(p2) + p3;
		});

		return emailAddress;
	}

}(jQuery));
//# sourceMappingURL=main.js.map
	
</script>

<script type="text/javascript">
	$(document).ready(function(){
		$("html").on("click", ".language-btn", function () {
			$(this).toggleClass("expand");
			$(".language-dropdown").toggleClass("expand");
		});

		$("html").on("click", "body", function (b) {
			if (!$(b.target).is(".language-btn")) {
				$(".language-btn").removeClass("expand");
				$(".language-dropdown").removeClass("expand");
			}
		});
	});
</script>

	var isProcessing = false; 
    function alter_ul_post_values(obj,post_id,ul_type){
	
		if (isProcessing)    
		return;  
		isProcessing = true;   
		var like_nonce = jQuery('#_wpnonce').val();
		jQuery(obj).find("span").html("..");
                jQuery.ajax({
                    type: "POST",
                    url: "https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/plugins/like-dislike-counter-for-posts-pages-and-comments/ajax_counter.php",
                    data: "post_id="+post_id+"&up_type="+ul_type+"&ul_nonce="+like_nonce,
                    success: function(msg){
                            jQuery(obj).find("span").html(msg);
                            isProcessing = false; 
                            jQuery(obj).find('svg').children('path').attr('stroke','#0050FF');
                            jQuery(obj).removeClass('idc_ul_cont_not_liked idc_ul_cont_not_liked_inner');
                    }
 		});
	}

function loadScript(url, defer){
    var script1 = document.createElement('script');
    script1.setAttribute('type', 'text/javascript');
    script1.setAttribute('src',url);
    if(defer == true){
        script1.setAttribute('defer','defer');
    }
    document.head.appendChild(script1);
}
function loadScript1(url, callback){

    var script = document.createElement("script")
    script.type = "text/javascript";
    if (script.readyState){  //IE
        script.onreadystatechange = function(){
            if (script.readyState == "loaded" || script.readyState == "complete"){
                script.onreadystatechange = null;
                callback();
            }
        };
    } else {  //Others
        script.onload = function(){
            callback();
        };
    }
    script.src = url;
    document.getElementsByTagName("head")[0].appendChild(script);
}

document.addEventListener("DOMContentLoaded", function() {
  // Find all the forms on the page
  var forms = document.getElementsByTagName("form");

  // Loop through each form
  for (var i = 0; i < forms.length; i++) {
    var form = forms[i];

    // Check if the hidden input field already exists
    var requestInput = form.querySelector("input[name='web_unit42_req_id']");
    if (!requestInput) {
      // Create a hidden input element if it doesn't exist
      requestInput = document.createElement("input");
      requestInput.type = "hidden";
      requestInput.name = "web_unit42_req_id";
      requestInput.id = "web_unit42_req_id";

      // Append the hidden field to the form
      form.appendChild(requestInput);
    }
  }
});

// Assign the onCheqResponse function to the window object to handle events from any form
window.onCheqResponse = function(encryptedMessage, requestID) {
  // Find all the forms on the page
  var forms = document.getElementsByTagName("form");

  for (var i = 0; i < forms.length; i++) {
    var form = forms[i];
    var requestInput = form.querySelector("input[name='web_unit42_req_id']");
    if (requestInput) {
      requestInput.value = requestID;
    }
  }
};
